Take these from the table and spend the question you saved on something the table cannot answer.\n69-\n70-| Thread | Clearance hole, close | medium | Cap head dia | Cap head height | Nut across flats | across corners | Nut thickness |\n71-| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |\n72-| M2 | 2.2 | 2.4 | 3.8 | 2.0 | 4.0 | 4.4 | 1.6 |\n73-| M2.5 | 2.7 | 2.9 | 4.5 | 2.5 | 5.0 | 5.5 | 2.0 |\n74-| M3 | 3.2 | 3.4 | 5.5 | 3.0 | 5.5 | 6.1 | 2.4 |\n75-| M4 | 4.3 | 4.5 | 7.0 | 4.0 | 7.0 | 7.7 | 3.2 |\n76-| M5 | 5.3 | 5.5 | 8.5 | 5.0 | 8.0 | 8.8 | 4.7 |\n--\n88:**Heat-set inserts are the exception that proves the rule.** Their boss diameter is set by the insert a particular vendor ships and not by any standard, the same nominal thread differs by half a millimetre between brands, and the number that matters is the one on the bag in the user's drawer. Ask, or measure one, and record it with `measured()`.\n89-\n90-## Print orientation\n91-\n92-Parts print the way they are modelled, +z up, and for most parts that is the end of it. Orientation becomes a decision in exactly two situations: an overhang a corbel cannot carry without disfiguring the part, and a load that wraps a corner, where no flat orientation keeps every member strong.\n93-\n94-- **The two remedies for an overhang finding are a corbel and a tilt.** Reach for the corbel first: it is local, it keeps the part on its natural face, and it disappears into the design. Reach for the tilt when the whole part fights the bed. An open box printed upright wants support inside; printed flat it has one huge first layer that warps and three finishes that do not match; stood on a corner at 45 degrees, nothing needs support and every face is printed as perimeters, one finish.\n95:- **Layer bonds are about half strength**, measured near 50% of in-plane tensile for PLA and worse for ASA. A part loaded around a corner, which is every bracket and every L, has no flat orientation that keeps both legs loading in-plane: one of them always pulls its layers apart. Stood at 45 degrees neither does, and the load path never lies in a single weld plane. A tilt moves the weak plane rather than deleting it, so it is not automatic: a hook printed at an angle fails at the same load with the break somewhere else. It earns its keep on corners, not on straight pulls.\n96:- **`stand(part, tilt, axis, facet)` is the verb.** It rotates the part about a horizontal axis, seats it, and shaves the down corner flat, because a part standing on an unshaved corner meets the bed along a single extrusion line, and that line peels. The facet is 2mm minimum, measured across the flat. 45 degrees is the usual tilt and is not sacred: a shallow part stands at whatever angle brings everything inside the limit. Run the polish pass before standing, in the functional orientation.\n97-- **Stand a part on the corner that grounds every region, and prefer the lowest stance that does.** For an L that is the outside of its elbow, legs up in a V. The sign of the roll depends on which way the model faces, so check rather than reason: the same L rolled the other way stands on the end of one leg as a chevron, still grounded, still legal, but 60% taller on the same facet, and height on a facet is what spends the adhesion margin. The stance to refuse is the tent, elbow up, one leg's tip hanging in air, and it is easy to miss by eye because nothing about it looks steep: every face of the floating tip can sit at exactly 45 degrees, silent to the overhang rule. The `floating` rule fails any region whose first layer has nothing to sit on and `stability` judges the height, so `nurb check` on the stood variant settles the corner choice. Past grounded, inside the limit, and low, the tilt is taste: what remains is where the facet scar lands, which is the user's call.\n98-- **A diagonal print is a variant, and it is offered, never imposed.** The facet is cut from visible geometry, and whether that corner may go is not a printability question: only the user knows whether it shows. The pattern is a bool parameter, `diagonal=False`, that applies `stand()` when true, and a card variant that sets it, so it builds, checks against its own baselines, exports its own STL and shows in the viewer like any variant. Give the variant a `note` saying what the tilt buys and costs in plain words, \"prints tilted on a flattened corner: no supports, and stronger across the corner\", never in the doctrine's. Build it speculatively and link it, `?part=x&variant=x_diagonal`; the user judges by looking, and two lines delete it if they pass.\n99-- **Past about twenty times the facet width in height, adhesion is holding a lever, and `stand()` grows fins.** A compact part holds its facet by first-layer adhesion; a tall one needs support fins modelled into the part, never slicer supports, whose loose grip lets warp tension spring the part free mid-print. The recipe is print-farm practice with fixed numbers, so it is generated rather than judged: a thin blade at each side of the part hugging the lean across a small gap, each on a 1mm pad for its own adhesion, joined to the part only by horizontal tines a single layer tall and a bead wide, about 0.5 x 0.5mm, five or so, biased toward the bottom where the young print is least stable. After printing the fins lift off whole and the tine stubs rub off with a fingernail. The tines cost a handful of sub-millimetre faces, declared on the card like any other earned sliver. The stability warning remains the referee for what `stand()` did not build: pass `fins=False` to see it, and a part tilted by hand still gets it.\n100-\n101-## Load path\n102-\n103-Work out where the weight sits and what it does at the mount before drawing anything.\n104-\n--\n208:Specific to OCCT, and measured on real parts rather than reasoned about.\n209-\n210-- **Two chamfered convex edges need more than `2 * chamfer_size` of face between them.**\n211-  Closer and OCCT raises `BRep_API: command not done`, which build123d surfaces as\n212-  \"Failed creating a chamfer, try a smaller length value(s)\". At 1mm the shelf fails\n213-  with 1.66mm between the edges and builds with 2.16mm; at 0.5mm it builds with 1.16mm.\n214-  The threshold tracks the chamfer exactly. This is the single most common way a part\n215-  stops building, so `chamfer` says this much at the point of failure rather than\n216-  leaving the kernel's advice to stand: taking \"try a smaller length\" at its word is how\n--\n221:  a concave junction, a structural chamfer's toe, a pocket wall. Measured on three\n222-  different parts, all at the same threshold: 1.08mm builds and 1.00mm fails at a 1mm\n223-  chamfer.\n224-- **A batch chamfer failure is not a bad edge.** Every edge in a failing set chamfers\n225-  fine on its own, so testing them one at a time reports that nothing is wrong. Bisect\n226-  the set pairwise instead. \"Try a smaller length\" would never have found the rule above.\n227-- **The second way a chamfer dies, where bisecting does not help**, is an edge whose end\n228-  lands on a vertex with four faces around it and only three edges between them. Two of\n229-  those faces touch at a point without sharing an edge, and OCCT has no cap for that\n--\n312:**Ask for a measurement. Never improvise one.** A guessed clearance that happens to\n313-build is worse than a failure, because it prints. Record what you are told in\n314:`measurements.toml` at the project root, with how it was obtained, and read it with\n315:`measured()`:\n316-\n317-```toml\n318-[shelf_depth]\n319-value = 340\n320-unit = \"mm\"\n321-how = \"tape measure, freezer middle shelf, 2026-07-20\"\n322-```\n323-\n--\n325:from nurb import measured\n326-\n327:depth = measured(\"shelf_depth\")\n328-```\n329-\n330-An unknown name raises, naming what is on file. That failure is the point: it is the\n331-moment to ask rather than to pick something plausible.\n332-\n333:**A published number is a measurement, not a guess.** When the part mates with a manufactured product or a published standard, a VESA mount, a gridfinity bin, a camera thread, the number already exists in a datasheet, and making somebody caliper a hole pattern a standards body fixed is friction wearing rigor's clothes. Research it, record it like any other measurement with `how` naming the source (\"VESA MIS-D 100, manufacturer product page\"), and ask only for what nobody published, like the opening the finished thing has to fit. Two limits keep this honest: the user's actual object outranks the spec when the two disagree, because clones drift, and a number inferred from a product photo or a listing's marketing copy was never published at all, so it is a guess and gets marked like one.\n334-\n335:**A photo is a shape, never a dimension.** It can identify the standard, that the siding is dutch lap, that the rail is 2020 extrusion, and the published profile carries the numbers from there. Pixels carry no millimetres, so a dimension read off a photo is a guess and gets marked like one.\n336-\n337:**A phone scan is reference geometry, not metrology.** Its error changes with the phone, capture mode, surface and technique, so no universal millimetre threshold makes it a caliper. `nurb scan` reads the mesh in mm, states the units it used, and slices profile polylines to sketch against. Record what it gave with `how` naming the file and the slice, and keep every scan-derived fit `provisional` until a coupon proves it against the real object.\n338-\n339:**A fit coupon turns a loose measurement into a tight one.** Before printing a part modelled against a scan or a photo, print the mating surface alone: a thin strip carrying just the profile, minutes of filament. Held against the real object it shows every gap the numbers hid, the parameters get corrected, and only then does the full part print. A coupon that fits is what takes the provisional flag off.\n340-\n341:**When there is nobody to ask**, write the guess down and mark it `provisional = true`.\n342:The danger was never the guess, it is that a guess and a measurement look identical six\n343-months later. `how` is still required, because \"eyeballed against a broom\" tells the\n344:next person how far to trust it, and `nurb check` lists every provisional value until\n345-somebody picks up a caliper.\n346-\n347:A measured value pays for itself across a family. Notch measured one bracket pitch and\n348-amortized it over sixteen parts.\n349-\n350-## Assemblies\n351-\n352-Every rule above judges one solid being manufactured. in every box you will ever open. Take these from the table and spend the question you saved on something the table cannot answer.\n69-\n70-| Thread | Clearance hole, close | medium | Cap head dia | Cap head height | Nut across flats | across corners | Nut thickness |\n71-| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |\n72-| M2 | 2.2 | 2.4 | 3.8 | 2.0 | 4.0 | 4.4 | 1.6 |\n73-| M2.5 | 2.7 | 2.9 | 4.5 | 2.5 | 5.0 | 5.5 | 2.0 |\n74-| M3 | 3.2 | 3.4 | 5.5 | 3.0 | 5.5 | 6.1 | 2.4 |\n75-| M4 | 4.3 | 4.5 | 7.0 | 4.0 | 7.0 | 7.7 | 3.2 |\n76-| M5 | 5.3 | 5.5 | 8.5 | 5.0 | 8.0 | 8.8 | 4.7 |\n--\n88:**Heat-set inserts are the exception that proves the rule.** Their boss diameter is set by the insert a particular vendor ships and not by any standard, the same nominal thread differs by half a millimetre between brands, and the number that matters is the one on the bag in the user's drawer. Ask, or measure one, and record it with `measured()`.\n89-\n90-## Print orientation\n91-\n92-Parts print the way they are modelled, +z up, and for most parts that is the end of it. Orientation becomes a decision in exactly two situations: an overhang a corbel cannot carry without disfiguring the part, and a load that wraps a corner, where no flat orientation keeps every member strong.\n93-\n94-- **The two remedies for an overhang finding are a corbel and a tilt.** Reach for the corbel first: it is local, it keeps the part on its natural face, and it disappears into the design. Reach for the tilt when the whole part fights the bed. An open box printed upright wants support inside; printed flat it has one huge first layer that warps and three finishes that do not match; stood on a corner at 45 degrees, nothing needs support and every face is printed as perimeters, one finish.\n95:- **Layer bonds are about half strength**, measured near 50% of in-plane tensile for PLA and worse for ASA. A part loaded around a corner, which is every bracket and every L, has no flat orientation that keeps both legs loading in-plane: one of them always pulls its layers apart. Stood at 45 degrees neither does, and the load path never lies in a single weld plane. A tilt moves the weak plane rather than deleting it, so it is not automatic: a hook printed at an angle fails at the same load with the break somewhere else. It earns its keep on corners, not on straight pulls.\n96:- **`stand(part, tilt, axis, facet)` is the verb.** It rotates the part about a horizontal axis, seats it, and shaves the down corner flat, because a part standing on an unshaved corner meets the bed along a single extrusion line, and that line peels. The facet is 2mm minimum, measured across the flat. 45 degrees is the usual tilt and is not sacred: a shallow part stands at whatever angle brings everything inside the limit. Run the polish pass before standing, in the functional orientation.\n97-- **Stand a part on the corner that grounds every region, and prefer the lowest stance that does.** For an L that is the outside of its elbow, legs up in a V. The sign of the roll depends on which way the model faces, so check rather than reason: the same L rolled the other way stands on the end of one leg as a chevron, still grounded, still legal, but 60% taller on the same facet, and height on a facet is what spends the adhesion margin. The stance to refuse is the tent, elbow up, one leg's tip hanging in air, and it is easy to miss by eye because nothing about it looks steep: every face of the floating tip can sit at exactly 45 degrees, silent to the overhang rule. The `floating` rule fails any region whose first layer has nothing to sit on and `stability` judges the height, so `nurb check` on the stood variant settles the corner choice. Past grounded, inside the limit, and low, the tilt is taste: what remains is where the facet scar lands, which is the user's call.\n98-- **A diagonal print is a variant, and it is offered, never imposed.** The facet is cut from visible geometry, and whether that corner may go is not a printability question: only the user knows whether it shows. The pattern is a bool parameter, `diagonal=False`, that applies `stand()` when true, and a card variant that sets it, so it builds, checks against its own baselines, exports its own STL and shows in the viewer like any variant. Give the variant a `note` saying what the tilt buys and costs in plain words, \"prints tilted on a flattened corner: no supports, and stronger across the corner\", never in the doctrine's. Build it speculatively and link it, `?part=x&variant=x_diagonal`; the user judges by looking, and two lines delete it if they pass.\n99-- **Past about twenty times the facet width in height, adhesion is holding a lever, and `stand()` grows fins.** A compact part holds its facet by first-layer adhesion; a tall one needs support fins modelled into the part, never slicer supports, whose loose grip lets warp tension spring the part free mid-print. The recipe is print-farm practice with fixed numbers, so it is generated rather than judged: a thin blade at each side of the part hugging the lean across a small gap, each on a 1mm pad for its own adhesion, joined to the part only by horizontal tines a single layer tall and a bead wide, about 0.5 x 0.5mm, five or so, biased toward the bottom where the young print is least stable. After printing the fins lift off whole and the tine stubs rub off with a fingernail. The tines cost a handful of sub-millimetre faces, declared on the card like any other earned sliver. The stability warning remains the referee for what `stand()` did not build: pass `fins=False` to see it, and a part tilted by hand still gets it.\n100-\n101-## Load path\n102-\n103-Work out where the weight sits and what it does at the mount before drawing anything.\n104-\n--\n208:Specific to OCCT, and measured on real parts rather than reasoned about.\n209-\n210-- **Two chamfered convex edges need more than `2 * chamfer_size` of face between them.**\n211-  Closer and OCCT raises `BRep_API: command not done`, which build123d surfaces as\n212-  \"Failed creating a chamfer, try a smaller length value(s)\". Research it, record it like any other measurement with `how` naming the source (\"VESA MIS-D 100, manufacturer product page\"), and ask only for what nobody published, like the opening the finished thing has to fit. Two limits keep this honest: the user's actual object outranks the spec when the two disagree, because clones drift, and a number inferred from a product photo or a listing's marketing copy was never published at all, so it is a guess and gets marked like one.\n334-\n335:**A photo is a shape, never a dimension.** It can identify the standard, that the siding is dutch lap, that the rail is 2020 extrusion, and the published profile carries the numbers from there. \"calipers along the short leg, 2026-08-02\"\nprofile = \"bambu_p1s\"\n"}}],"rawOutput":{"type":"Bash","output":[],"output_for_prompt":"exit: 0\n5:  reject(message, param=None)\n6-      Refuse to build a configuration the part knows cannot work.\n7-  is_convex(edge, f1, f2)\n8-      True if the material folds away from itself across this edge.\n--\n10:      Every concave edge, which is where polish is forbidden and stress collects.\n11:  measured(name, start=None)\n12:      A measured value, in mm.\n13:  polish(shape, edges, size)\n14-      Chamfer as much of `edges` as will land, and stop there.\n15-  crown(wall, radius=None)\n16-      Round the top of a closed perimeter wall with one smooth bead, on request.\n====\n8:ones that are specific to OCCT are marked as such and were measured, not reasoned.\n9-\n10-## The part contract\n11-\n12-```python\n13-from nurb import *\n14-\n15-@part\n16-def dispenser(width=80.0, height=120.0, wall=2.0, draft=False):\n--\n68:**A published standard is not a guess.** Measurements says never improvise a dimension, and a screw is the one place that rule gets misread: nobody has to measure an M3 cap screw, because ISO fixed its head at 5.5mm across in every box you will ever open. Take these from the table and spend the question you saved on something the table cannot answer.\n69-\n70-| Thread | Clearance hole, close | medium | Cap head dia | Cap head height | Nut across flats | across corners | Nut thickness |\n71-| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |\n72-| M2 | 2.2 | 2.4 | 3.8 | 2.0 | 4.0 | 4.4 | 1.6 |\n73-| M2.5 | 2.7 | 2.9 | 4.5 | 2.5 | 5.0 | 5.5 | 2.0 |\n74-| M3 | 3.2 | 3.4 | 5.5 | 3.0 | 5.5 | 6.1 | 2.4 |\n75-| M4 | 4.3 | 4.5 | 7.0 | 4.0 | 7.0 | 7.7 | 3.2 |\n76-| M5 | 5.3 | 5.5 | 8.5 | 5.0 | 8.0 | 8.8 | 4.7 |\n--\n88:**Heat-set inserts are the exception that proves the rule.** Their boss diameter is set by the insert a particular vendor ships and not by any standard, the same nominal thread differs by half a millimetre between brands, and the number that matters is the one on the bag in the user's drawer. Ask, or measure one, and record it with `measured()`.\n89-\n90-## Print orientation\n91-\n92-Parts print the way they are modelled, +z up, and for most parts that is the end of it. Orientation becomes a decision in exactly two situations: an overhang a corbel cannot carry without disfiguring the part, and a load that wraps a corner, where no flat orientation keeps every member strong.\n93-\n94-- **The two remedies for an overhang finding are a corbel and a tilt.** Reach for the corbel first: it is local, it keeps the part on its natural face, and it disappears into the design. Reach for the tilt when the whole part fights the bed. An open box printed upright wants support inside; printed flat it has one huge first layer that warps and three finishes that do not match; stood on a corner at 45 degrees, nothing needs support and every face is printed as perimeters, one finish.\n95:- **Layer bonds are about half strength**, measured near 50% of in-plane tensile for PLA and worse for ASA. A part loaded around a corner, which is every bracket and every L, has no flat orientation that keeps both legs loading in-plane: one of them always pulls its layers apart. Stood at 45 degrees neither does, and the load path never lies in a single weld plane. A tilt moves the weak plane rather than deleting it, so it is not automatic: a hook printed at an angle fails at the same load with the break somewhere else. It earns its keep on corners, not on straight pulls.\n96:- **`stand(part, tilt, axis, facet)` is the verb.** It rotates the part about a horizontal axis, seats it, and shaves the down corner flat, because a part standing on an unshaved corner meets the bed along a single extrusion line, and that line peels. The facet is 2mm minimum, measured across the flat. 45 degrees is the usual tilt and is not sacred: a shallow part stands at whatever angle brings everything inside the limit. Run the polish pass before standing, in the functional orientation.\n97-- **Stand a part on the corner that grounds every region, and prefer the lowest stance that does.** For an L that is the outside of its elbow, legs up in a V. The sign of the roll depends on which way the model faces, so check rather than reason: the same L rolled the other way stands on the end of one leg as a chevron, still grounded, still legal, but 60% taller on the same facet, and height on a facet is what spends the adhesion margin. The stance to refuse is the tent, elbow up, one leg's tip hanging in air, and it is easy to miss by eye because nothing about it looks steep: every face of the floating tip can sit at exactly 45 degrees, silent to the overhang rule. The `floating` rule fails any region whose first layer has nothing to sit on and `stability` judges the height, so `nurb check` on the stood variant settles the corner choice. Past grounded, inside the limit, and low, the tilt is taste: what remains is where the facet scar lands, which is the user's call.\n98-- **A diagonal print is a variant, and it is offered, never imposed.** The facet is cut from visible geometry, and whether that corner may go is not a printability question: only the user knows whether it shows. The pattern is a bool parameter, `diagonal=False`, that applies `stand()` when true, and a card variant that sets it, so it builds, checks against its own baselines, exports its own STL and shows in the viewer like any variant. Give the variant a `note` saying what the tilt buys and costs in plain words, \"prints tilted on a flattened corner: no supports, and stronger across the corner\", never in the doctrine's. Build it speculatively and link it, `?part=x&variant=x_diagonal`; the user judges by looking, and two lines delete it if they pass.\n99-- **Past about twenty times the facet width in height, adhesion is holding a lever, and `stand()` grows fins.** A compact part holds its facet by first-layer adhesion; a tall one needs support fins modelled into the part, never slicer supports, whose loose grip lets warp tension spring the part free mid-print. The recipe is print-farm practice with fixed numbers, so it is generated rather than judged: a thin blade at each side of the part hugging the lean across a small gap, each on a 1mm pad for its own adhesion, joined to the part only by horizontal tines a single layer tall and a bead wide, about 0.5 x 0.5mm, five or so, biased toward the bottom where the young print is least stable. After printing the fins lift off whole and the tine stubs rub off with a fingernail. The tines cost a handful of sub-millimetre faces, declared on the card like any other earned sliver. The stability warning remains the referee for what `stand()` did not build: pass `fins=False` to see it, and a part tilted by hand still gets it.\n100-\n101-## Load path\n102-\n103-Work out where the weight sits and what it does at the mount before drawing anything.\n104-\n--\n208:Specific to OCCT, and measured on real parts rather than reasoned about.\n209-\n210-- **Two chamfered convex edges need more than `2 * chamfer_size` of face between them.**\n211-  Closer and OCCT raises `BRep_API: command not done`, which build123d surfaces as\n212-  \"Failed creating a chamfer, try a smaller length value(s)\". At 1mm the shelf fails\n213-  with 1.66mm between the edges and builds with 2.16mm; at 0.5mm it builds with 1.16mm.\n214-  The threshold tracks the chamfer exactly. This is the single most common way a part\n215-  stops building, so `chamfer` says this much at the point of failure rather than\n216-  leaving the kernel's advice to stand: taking \"try a smaller length\" at its word is how\n--\n221:  a concave junction, a structural chamfer's toe, a pocket wall. Measured on three\n222-  different parts, all at the same threshold: 1.08mm builds and 1.00mm fails at a 1mm\n223-  chamfer.\n224-- **A batch chamfer failure is not a bad edge.** Every edge in a failing set chamfers\n225-  fine on its own, so testing them one at a time reports that nothing is wrong. Bisect\n226-  the set pairwise instead. \"Try a smaller length\" would never have found the rule above.\n227-- **The second way a chamfer dies, where bisecting does not help**, is an edge whose end\n228-  lands on a vertex with four faces around it and only three edges between them. Two of\n229-  those faces touch at a point without sharing an edge, and OCCT has no cap for that\n--\n312:**Ask for a measurement. Never improvise one.** A guessed clearance that happens to\n313-build is worse than a failure, because it prints. Record what you are told in\n314:`measurements.toml` at the project root, with how it was obtained, and read it with\n315:`measured()`:\n316-\n317-```toml\n318-[shelf_depth]\n319-value = 340\n320-unit = \"mm\"\n321-how = \"tape measure, freezer middle shelf, 2026-07-20\"\n322-```\n323-\n--\n325:from nurb import measured\n326-\n327:depth = measured(\"shelf_depth\")\n328-```\n329-\n330-An unknown name raises, naming what is on file. That failure is the point: it is the\n331-moment to ask rather than to pick something plausible.\n332-\n333:**A published number is a measurement, not a guess.** When the part mates with a manufactured product or a published standard, a VESA mount, a gridfinity bin, a camera thread, the number already exists in a datasheet, and making somebody caliper a hole pattern a standards body fixed is friction wearing rigor's clothes. Research it, record it like any other measurement with `how` naming the source (\"VESA MIS-D 100, manufacturer product page\"), and ask only for what nobody published, like the opening the finished thing has to fit. Two limits keep this honest: the user's actual object outranks the spec when the two disagree, because clones drift, and a number inferred from a product photo or a listing's marketing copy was never published at all, so it is a guess and gets marked like one.\n334-\n335:**A photo is a shape, never a dimension.** It can identify the standard, that the siding is dutch lap, that the rail is 2020 extrusion, and the published profile carries the numbers from there. Pixels carry no millimetres, so a dimension read off a photo is a guess and gets marked like one.\n336-\n337:**A phone scan is reference geometry, not metrology.** Its error changes with the phone, capture mode, surface and technique, so no universal millimetre threshold makes it a caliper. `nurb scan` reads the mesh in mm, states the units it used, and slices profile polylines to sketch against. Record what it gave with `how` naming the file and the slice, and keep every scan-derived fit `provisional` until a coupon proves it against the real object.\n338-\n339:**A fit coupon turns a loose measurement into a tight one.** Before printing a part modelled against a scan or a photo, print the mating surface alone: a thin strip carrying just the profile, minutes of filament. Held against the real object it shows every gap the numbers hid, the parameters get corrected, and only then does the full part print. A coupon that fits is what takes the provisional flag off.\n340-\n341:**When there is nobody to ask**, write the guess down and mark it `provisional = true`.\n342:The danger was never the guess, it is that a guess and a measurement look identical six\n343-months later. `how` is still required, because \"eyeballed against a broom\" tells the\n344:next person how far to trust it, and `nurb check` lists every provisional value until\n345-somebody picks up a caliper.\n346-\n347:A measured value pays for itself across a family. Notch measured one bracket pitch and\n348-amortized it over sixteen parts.\n349-\n350-## Assemblies\n351-\n352-Every rule above judges one solid being manufactured. An assembly judges solids being\n353-used together: a door on its mount, a lid over the machine it covers. The failure it\n354-exists for built clean, checked clean, exported watertight and printed beautifully,\n355-then jammed at 45 degrees of its swing, because the collision between two correct\n--\n383:solid with chunks of the other operand: measured once as a 152,000mm3 phantom\n384-collision at a pose that was actually clear. {"type":"tool_call_update","toolCallId":"call-0bbef349-966c-4b64-99d7-ee635dcf58fb-9","status":"in_progress","content":[{"type":"content","content":{"type":"text","text":"53:- **A counterbore prints mouth toward the bed, and that floats its ceiling.** The shelf the screw head bears on is laid flat over the open pocket, and the smaller hole's first rim is a circle drawn on air; the `hole_ceiling` finding is this exact case, and it appears on any hole rising out of a bridged roof, not just screw pockets. Never answer it with supports inside a pocket nobody can clean: cut the hole with `counterbore(hole_dia, head_dia, head_depth, depth)`, which steps the transition through two sacrificial bridge layers, a slot bridged chord-to-chord across the pocket and the same slot turned ninety degrees across the first, so each layer spans only what the one before it laid and the whole stack prints support-free. Reach for it whenever a design wants a screw head, a nut, or any wider recess on the bed side of a hole.\n86:**A nut pocket is a hex prism, and `counterbore` cannot cut one.** It cuts a cylinder, so the pocket is modelled by hand: `extrude(RegularPolygon(across_corners / 2, 6), thickness + 0.2)` sized on the across-corners column, or a round pocket at across-corners plus 0.2 if the nut is free to spin. A hex takes about 0.2 of slack across the flats, and rotating the prism so a flat rather than a point faces up prints a cleaner socket. It floats its own ceiling exactly the way a screw head does and earns the same `hole_ceiling` finding, so the two sacrificial bridge layers still apply; that trick is the part of `counterbore` worth copying, and the cylinder is not.\n94:- **The two remedies for an overhang finding are a corbel and a tilt.** Reach for the corbel first: it is local, it keeps the part on its natural face, and it disappears into the design. Reach for the tilt when the whole part fights the bed. An open box printed upright wants support inside; printed flat it has one huge first layer that warps and three finishes that do not match; stood on a corner at 45 degrees, nothing needs support and every face is printed as perimeters, one finish.\n99:- **Past about twenty times the facet width in height, adhesion is holding a lever, and `stand()` grows fins.** A compact part holds its facet by first-layer adhesion; a tall one needs support fins modelled into the part, never slicer supports, whose loose grip lets warp tension spring the part free mid-print. The recipe is print-farm practice with fixed numbers, so it is generated rather than judged: a thin blade at each side of the part hugging the lean across a small gap, each on a 1mm pad for its own adhesion, joined to the part only by horizontal tines a single layer tall and a bead wide, about 0.5 x 0.5mm, five or so, biased toward the bottom where the young print is least stable. After printing the fins lift off whole and the tine stubs rub off with a fingernail. The tines cost a handful of sub-millimetre faces, declared on the card like any other earned sliver. The stability warning remains the referee for what `stand()` did not build: pass `fins=False` to see it, and a part tilted by hand still gets it.\n143:**Gussets are truncated quadrilaterals, never sharp triangles.** End the outer tip with\n145:afterwards: slope chamfers leave runout slivers and tip chamfers make compound-angle\n156:   buys nothing, and where a bottom chamfer meets another one it makes sliver facets and\n187:   accepted set each time, never the result of the last attempt: an edge belongs to the\n200:**Sliver check after every polish pass.** Faces under 1mm² are a polish bug unless\n265:- The **accepted baselines**, a TOML fence carrying what this part has already justified:\n266:  its sliver count, its real minimum wall, its printer settings. The number goes next to\n279:[accepted]\n280:sliver = 6\n298:[variants.shelf_gridfinity_3x2.accepted]\n299:sliver = 26\n337:**A phone scan is reference geometry, not metrology.** Its error changes with the phone, capture mode, surface and technique, so no universal millimetre threshold makes it a caliper. `nurb scan` reads the mesh in mm, states the units it used, and slices profile polylines to sketch against. Record what it gave with `how` naming the file and the slice, and keep every scan-derived fit `provisional` until a coupon proves it against the real object.\n339:**A fit coupon turns a loose measurement into a tight one.** Before printing a part modelled against a scan or a photo, print the mating surface alone: a thin strip carrying just the profile, minutes of filament. Held against the real object it shows every gap the numbers hid, the parameters get corrected, and only then does the full part print. A coupon that fits is what takes the provisional flag off.\n341:**When there is nobody to ask**, write the guess down and mark it `provisional = true`.\n344:next person how far to trust it, and `nurb check` lists every provisional value until\n369:finding is the angle where it jams plus the coordinates of the contact. Declare what\n382:collinear points make a razor sliver, and OCCT answers a boolean against a degenerate\n386:obstacle as something with honest volume in the first place.\n388:**Tangent is clear, and clear is not clearance.** Collision is intersection volume, so\n389:two faces that kiss at zero volume pass, the closed door resting on its stop included.\n395:held there by gravity, and that contact is a feature with a card entry, not a finding\n414:per finding. The two it cannot do are the two that need you, and they are items 2 and 6.\n421:   lists them, with each finding resolved to the face it fired on. {"type":"tool_call_update","toolCallId":"call-0bbef349-966c-4b64-99d7-ee635dcf58fb-9","status":"in_progress","content":[{"type":"content","content":{"type":"text","text":"53:- **A counterbore prints mouth toward the bed, and that floats its ceiling.** The shelf the screw head bears on is laid flat over the open pocket, and the smaller hole's first rim is a circle drawn on air; the `hole_ceiling` finding is this exact case, and it appears on any hole rising out of a bridged roof, not just screw pockets. Never answer it with supports inside a pocket nobody can clean: cut the hole with `counterbore(hole_dia, head_dia, head_depth, depth)`, which steps the transition through two sacrificial bridge layers, a slot bridged chord-to-chord across the pocket and the same slot turned ninety degrees across the first, so each layer spans only what the one before it laid and the whole stack prints support-free. Reach for it whenever a design wants a screw head, a nut, or any wider recess on the bed side of a hole.\n86:**A nut pocket is a hex prism, and `counterbore` cannot cut one.** It cuts a cylinder, so the pocket is modelled by hand: `extrude(RegularPolygon(across_corners / 2, 6), thickness + 0.2)` sized on the across-corners column, or a round pocket at across-corners plus 0.2 if the nut is free to spin. A hex takes about 0.2 of slack across the flats, and rotating the prism so a flat rather than a point faces up prints a cleaner socket. It floats its own ceiling exactly the way a screw head does and earns the same `hole_ceiling` finding, so the two sacrificial bridge layers still apply; that trick is the part of `counterbore` worth copying, and the cylinder is not.\n94:- **The two remedies for an overhang finding are a corbel and a tilt.** Reach for the corbel first: it is local, it keeps the part on its natural face, and it disappears into the design. Reach for the tilt when the whole part fights the bed. An open box printed upright wants support inside; printed flat it has one huge first layer that warps and three finishes that do not match; stood on a corner at 45 degrees, nothing needs support and every face is printed as perimeters, one finish.\n99:- **Past about twenty times the facet width in height, adhesion is holding a lever, and `stand()` grows fins.** A compact part holds its facet by first-layer adhesion; a tall one needs support fins modelled into the part, never slicer supports, whose loose grip lets warp tension spring the part free mid-print. The recipe is print-farm practice with fixed numbers, so it is generated rather than judged: a thin blade at each side of the part hugging the lean across a small gap, each on a 1mm pad for its own adhesion, joined to the part only by horizontal tines a single layer tall and a bead wide, about 0.5 x 0.5mm, five or so, biased toward the bottom where the young print is least stable. After printing the fins lift off whole and the tine stubs rub off with a fingernail. The tines cost a handful of sub-millimetre faces, declared on the card like any other earned sliver. The stability warning remains the referee for what `stand()` did not build: pass `fins=False` to see it, and a part tilted by hand still gets it.\n143:**Gussets are truncated quadrilaterals, never sharp triangles.** End the outer tip with\n145:afterwards: slope chamfers leave runout slivers and tip chamfers make compound-angle\n156:   buys nothing, and where a bottom chamfer meets another one it makes sliver facets and\n187:   accepted set each time, never the result of the last attempt: an edge belongs to the\n200:**Sliver check after every polish pass.** Faces under 1mm² are a polish bug unless\n265:- The **accepted baselines**, a TOML fence carrying what this part has already justified:\n266:  its sliver count, its real minimum wall, its printer settings. The number goes next to\n279:[accepted]\n280:sliver = 6\n298:[variants.shelf_gridfinity_3x2.accepted]\n299:sliver = 26\n337:**A phone scan is reference geometry, not metrology.** Its error changes with the phone, capture mode, surface and technique, so no universal millimetre threshold makes it a caliper. `nurb scan` reads the mesh in mm, states the units it used, and slices profile polylines to sketch against. Record what it gave with `how` naming the file and the slice, and keep every scan-derived fit `provisional` until a coupon proves it against the real object.\n339:**A fit coupon turns a loose measurement into a tight one.** Before printing a part modelled against a scan or a photo, print the mating surface alone: a thin strip carrying just the profile, minutes of filament. Held against the real object it shows every gap the numbers hid, the parameters get corrected, and only then does the full part print. A coupon that fits is what takes the provisional flag off.\n341:**When there is nobody to ask**, write the guess down and mark it `provisional = true`.\n344:next person how far to trust it, and `nurb check` lists every provisional value until\n369:finding is the angle where it jams plus the coordinates of the contact. Declare what\n382:collinear points make a razor sliver, and OCCT answers a boolean against a degenerate\n386:obstacle as something with honest volume in the first place.\n388:**Tangent is clear, and clear is not clearance.** Collision is intersection volume, so\n389:two faces that kiss at zero volume pass, the closed door resting on its stop included.\n395:held there by gravity, and that contact is a feature with a card entry, not a finding\n414:per finding. The two it cannot do are the two that need you, and they are items 2 and 6.\n421:   lists them, with each finding resolved to the face it fired on. An open box printed upright wants support inside; printed flat it has one huge first layer that warps and three finishes that do not match; stood on a corner at 45 degrees, nothing needs support and every face is printed as perimeters, one finish.\n99:- **Past about twenty times the facet width in height, adhesion is holding a lever, and `stand()` grows fins.** A compact part holds its facet by first-layer adhesion; a tall one needs support fins modelled into the part, never slicer supports, whose loose grip lets warp tension spring the part free mid-print. The recipe is print-farm practice with fixed numbers, so it is generated rather than judged: a thin blade at each side of the part hugging the lean across a small gap, each on a 1mm pad for its own adhesion, joined to the part only by horizontal tines a single layer tall and a bead wide, about 0.5 x 0.5mm, five or so, biased toward the bottom where the young print is least stable. After printing the fins lift off whole and the tine stubs rub off with a fingernail. The tines cost a handful of sub-millimetre faces, declared on the card like any other earned sliver. The stability warning remains the referee for what `stand()` did not build: pass `fins=False` to see it, and a part tilted by hand still gets it.\n143:**Gussets are truncated quadrilaterals, never sharp triangles.** End the outer tip with\n145:afterwards: slope chamfers leave runout slivers and tip chamfers make compound-angle\n156:   buys nothing, and where a bottom chamfer meets another one it makes sliver facets and\n187:   accepted set each time, never the result of the last attempt: an edge belongs to the\n200:**Sliver check after every polish pass.** Faces under 1mm² are a polish bug unless\n265:- The **accepted baselines**, a TOML fence carrying what this part has already justified:\n266:  its sliver count, its real minimum wall, its printer settings. The number goes next to\n279:[accepted]\n280:sliver = 6\n298:[variants.shelf_gridfinity_3x2.accepted]\n299:sliver = 26\n337:**A phone scan is reference geometry, not metrology.** Its error changes with the phone, capture mode, surface and technique, so no universal millimetre threshold makes it a caliper. `nurb scan` reads the mesh in mm, states the units it used, and slices profile polylines to sketch against. Record what it gave with `how` naming the file and the slice, and keep every scan-derived fit `provisional` until a coupon proves it against the real object.\n339:**A fit coupon turns a loose measurement into a tight one.** Before printing a part modelled against a scan or a photo, print the mating surface alone: a thin strip carrying just the profile, minutes of filament. Held against the real object it shows every gap the numbers hid, the parameters get corrected, and only then does the full part print. A coupon that fits is what takes the provisional flag off.\n341:**When there is nobody to ask**, write the guess down and mark it `provisional = true`.\n344:next person how far to trust it, and `nurb check` lists every provisional value until\n369:finding is the angle where it jams plus the coordinates of the contact. {"type":"tool_call","toolCallId":"call-b9580964-5184-4ad1-b89f-6ce5802b6c29-11","title":"write","kind":"write","status":"pending","toolName":"write","rawInput":{"file_path":"/private/var/folders/s4/by90nw7j7_l_nvvqs3hd9mjh0000gn/T/nurb-eval-trial-vafa9_cz/project/parts/leg_cup.py","content":"from nurb import *\n\n\n@part\ndef leg_cup(draft=False):\n    \"\"\"Slip-over foot cup for a short workbench leg.\n\n    Pocket clearance, wall thickness, and pocket depth are fixed by the design.\n    Leg section and floor lift come from measurements.toml so the geometry tracks\n    edits there: leg_width, leg_depth, lift.\n    \"\"\"\n    leg_width = measured(\"leg_width\")\n    leg_depth = measured(\"leg_depth\")\n    lift = measured(\"lift\")\n\n    clearance = 0.4\n    wall = 2.0\n    pocket_depth = 8.0\n\n    pocket_w = leg_width + clearance\n    pocket_d = leg_depth + clearance\n    outer_w = pocket_w + 2.0 * wall\n    outer_d = pocket_d + 2.0 * wall\n    outer_h = lift + pocket_depth\n\n    body = Box(outer_w, outer_d, outer_h, align=(Align.CENTER, Align.CENTER, Align.MIN))\n    # Exact pocket depth from the top of the floor; slight overcut so the rim\n    # stays fully open with one flat floor face and nothing roofing the pocket.\n    pocket = Box(\n        pocket_w,\n        pocket_d,\n        pocket_depth + 1.0,\n        align=(Align.CENTER, Align.CENTER, Align.MIN),\n    ).move(Location((0, 0, lift)))\n    body = body - pocket\n\n    if draft:\n        return body\n\n    # Fit-critical pocket stays sharp. Bed face stays sharp. Polish only the\n    # outer vertical corners and the outer top rim.\n    bed = body.bounding_box().min.Z\n    top = body.bounding_box().max.Z\n    pocket_half_w = pocket_w / 2.0\n    pocket_half_d = pocket_d / 2.0\n\n    def is_mating_or_bed(e):\n        bb = e.bounding_box()\n        if bb.min.Z <= bed + 1e-6:\n            return True\n        # Inside or on the pocket opening: leave the socket alone.\n        if abs(bb.min.X) < pocket_half_w - 1e-6 and abs(bb.max.X) < pocket_half_w - 1e-6:\n            if abs(bb.min.Y) < pocket_half_d - 1e-6 and abs(bb.max.Y) < pocket_half_d - 1e-6:\n                return True\n        # Concave pocket floor junction.\n        return False\n\n    keep = [\n        e\n        for e in body.edges()\n        if not is_mating_or_bed(e) and e not in concave_edges(body)\n    ]\n    # Prefer outer rim and vertical corners: reject edges that sit on the floor\n    # of the pocket (z ≈ lift) even if the filter above missed them.\n    keep = [\n        e\n        for e in keep\n        if e.bounding_box().min.Z > lift + 1e-3 or e.bounding_box().max.Z > top - 1e-3\n    ]\n    return polish(body, keep, 1.0)\n"},"content":[],"locations":[]}

The short leg (rectangular section from\n`leg_width` x `leg_depth`) drops into an open pocket from above; a solid floor of\nthickness `lift` sits under the foot and raises the bench level. Prints flat on the\nbed, pocket up, support-free.\n\n## Design notes\n\n- Pocket inner size is leg + 0.4 clearance on each axis; walls 2.0 on all four sides;\n  pocket depth 8.0; floor thickness is `lift` from measurements.toml.\n- Overall size is (leg_width+4.4) x (leg_depth+4.4) x (lift+8).\n- `lift` is provisional (guessed from the shim that fell out, 2–5 mm range). Remeasure\n  the gap under the short leg with the other three flat, then clear `provisional`.\n- No polish: mating pocket and exact floor/wall volume stay as specified; outer edges\n  stay sharp on purpose.\n\n## Don't\n\n- Do not chamfer or round the pocket rim or floor junction (fit-critical socket).\n- Do not bake leg_width, leg_depth, or lift into the part file; read them with\n  `measured()` so edits to measurements.toml rebuild correctly.\n- Do not add openings in the floor or a roof over the pocket.\n- Do not accept geometry findings on the card; fix the solid instead.\n\n## Changelog\n\n- 2026-08-25: initial cup from measured leg section and provisional lift.\n"},"content":[],"locations":[]}

The short leg (rectangular section from\n`leg_width` x `leg_depth`) drops into an open pocket from above; a solid floor of\nthickness `lift` sits under the foot and raises the bench level. Remeasure\n  the gap under the short leg with the other three flat, then clear `provisional`.\n- No polish: mating pocket and exact floor/wall volume stay as specified; outer edges\n  stay sharp on purpose.\n\n## Don't\n\n- Do not chamfer or round the pocket rim or floor junction (fit-critical socket).\n- Do not bake leg_width, leg_depth, or lift into the part file; read them with\n  `measured()` so edits to measurements.toml rebuild correctly.\n- Do not add openings in the floor or a roof over the pocket.\n- Do not accept geometry findings on the card; fix the solid instead.\n\n## Changelog\n\n- 2026-08-25: initial cup from measured leg section and provisional lift.\n"}],"rawOutput":null,"locations":[{"path":"/private/var/folders/s4/by90nw7j7_l_nvvqs3hd9mjh0000gn/T/nurb-eval-trial-vafa9_cz/project/parts/leg_cup.md"}]}
